# Changelog

## 1.79.38

**Tense follows the clock.** The fifth critique opened Sprint 23, which ended on Jul 31, and read *Pace vs clock −40 pp · 100% elapsed · 40 percentage points behind the clock*, *Likely to carry over 6 items* and a verdict saying the sprint *is 100% elapsed*; opened the cross-team roll-up and read *Sprint health* beside *no single sprint clock* and *Likely to carry over 32 items*; and found the predictability chart drawing the running sprint's *67%* like a finished sprint's and folding it into *Average hit rate 82%*. Three vocabularies for three states. A sprint that has ended — closed at the source, or with an as-of date past its end — is described in the past tense: *Carried over*, *sprint over · finished 46 percentage points short*, *The sprint has ended: delivery finished 46 percentage points short of its scope*. The composite is named by what it spans: *Sprint health* for one sprint, *Roll-up health* across a board's sprints, *Portfolio health* across a project's boards, *Flow health* on a board without sprints — the name being the guard against comparing scores that were never measured the same way — and a roll-up's open work is *Still open*. And the sprint still running is drawn hollow and dotted in the predictability chart, distinct from the dashed rebuilt rows, left out of the average, and the note says so: *Average hit rate 85% over 5 finished sprints, the running one left out*. `tests/e2e.py` holds the demo's running sprint, a closed sprint from the bundle, a board roll-up and the cross-team roll-up.

## 1.79.37

**The value card's basis sentence describes the rule that counted the rows above it.** Beneath a priced Bug and a priced Story the card said *Value is recorded on epics and above, so work below that level is not counted here and is not missing from it.* Both were below that level and both were counted, by the rule [ADR 0025](docs/adr/0025-the-app-declares-a-business-value-field.md) sets for an issue with no recorded level — which is every issue in every file that records no hierarchy, which is every CSV. The fifth critique put it plainly: the one line a reader is told to challenge was wrong about the two rows above it. The sentence now follows the data: where nothing in view carries a hierarchy level it says *This file records no hierarchy, so value is counted on whichever items carry it, once each*; where levels exist the epic sentence stands. `tests/e2e.py` holds both, the second by giving the demo's priced issues a level.

## 1.79.36

**Merge needs only the key.** Step 2's Merge radio says *use this to layer a value-estimate file on top of a Jira export* and step 3's warning says the same; pasting exactly that — the repo's own `key,businessValue,valueBasis` template — with Merge chosen was refused by the wizard's own validation: *Pick a column for: Summary, Status, Created date. The dashboard cannot count without them.* The fifth critique called it a support ticket on day one. The required-column check had no notion of the mode. Under Merge the key is the only required column, because a matching row keeps every value the file does not carry — which is what the radio already promised and what `assemble()` already did. The mapping table now reads the radio live and marks the other required fields *kept from what is loaded* the moment Merge is chosen; a merge with no key column is still refused, with a reason about matching rather than counting; and the sprint-window fields offer the loaded sprint's name and dates the moment Merge is chosen, because a merge does not move the sprint — they had offered *Imported 2026-08-03* for a file with no dates, and a reader who took the suggestion renamed the sprint. Replace is unchanged. `tests/e2e.py` pastes a two-row value file, holds the Replace refusal, switches to Merge, holds the three re-marked rows, applies, and checks that the two issues took their values and kept their summaries.

## 1.79.35

**The band says less under a filter, because it knows less.** Three figures on the KPI band kept stating the whole sprint while the filters narrowed everything around them. The Delivered delta reads the sprint record and compares whole sprints, so under a Person filter *50% · 3 of 6 items* sat over *▲ 12 done vs 8 last sprint*, and on a cross-team roll-up *115 of 147 items* sat over *▼ 8 done vs 9 last sprint* — one tile, two bases, no label; it is drawn now only when nothing narrows the selection and one sprint is in view. A Status filter decides which items are on the page, so the share done is the filter's and not the sprint's: *Status = Done* read *100% · Pace vs clock +40 pp* and a green chip at 78/100, all by construction; pace is now withheld under a Status filter (*not measured under a Status filter*), the verdict no longer calls that share *tracking with the clock*, and the health score refuses, naming the filter. And a Person filter turned the sprint's score into one person's — *On track (75/100)* for one name against the team's amber 52 — which is one dropdown from the ranking of individuals this page does not produce; the chip refuses there too, and says it is *withheld by rule, not for want of evidence*, because that refusal is not about missing data and should not claim to be. Clear the filters and all three are back. `tests/e2e.py` walks a Person filter, a Status filter, the clear, and a roll-up.

## 1.79.34

**Value is not counted under a filter.** The fifth critique filtered the page to one person and read *Value closed $87,000 · 2 of 3 closed items priced*, with one of the two priced items owned by somebody else; filtered to one epic it read *2 of 1 closed items priced*; and the verdict repeated the $87,000 under a basis line that said *matching the current filters*. Value is counted on a different set from the items on purpose — epics, finished inside the sprint's window, once each ([ADR 0026](docs/adr/0026-items-and-value-are-counted-from-two-different-sets.md)) — and the Person, Epic, Types, Status and Find filters select items, so the tile's numerator came from one set and its denominator from another. A ratio over two sets is not a figure, and this one sat on the executive's scan line. Under any filter the Value tile now withholds its figure (*not counted under a filter*), the value card refuses in the tool's words — *Value is recorded on epics and counted once per epic, and the filters select items … the evidence is absent, not noisy* — the verdict carries no value finding, and the footer names value beside the five tiles that do not follow the filters. Clear the filters and the figure is back, unchanged. The facts pack is unaffected: it has no filters. `tests/e2e.py` sets a Person filter and a Status filter and holds the tile, the card, the verdict and the footer, then clears them and holds the figure's return.

## 1.79.33

**The toolbar's one row holds under a wider font.** 1.79.30 kept the toolbar to one row at 1280 wide by letting the title block shrink — and CI, whose Linux Chromium sets a wider face than the macOS system font, measured 131px on the same commit that measured 101px here, because the action group's own width is what the flex line is laid out against and on that machine it no longer fit beside a 300px title. The actions now claim free space first, up to their own width, from a basis well under it, and the title takes what is left and wraps its sub-line; where even that is too wide the buttons wrap among themselves instead of the whole group dropping to a second row. `tests/e2e.py` measures the toolbar at 1280 under the page's own font and again with a wide face forced, so the check no longer depends on which machine runs it.

## 1.79.32

**Per-person bars are in alphabetical order.** *Where each person's work sits* sorted its rows by total, descending. The card exists to show where a person's items stand, and `CLAUDE.md` allows exactly that — *ownership counts are fine; league tables are not* — but a list of people ordered by how much each has is a league table with the numbers written beside the names, one sort key from the ranking of individuals the product refuses to compute. The fourth critique asked whether alphabetical order was a constraint worth writing down; it is, and `DESIGN.md`'s don'ts now carry it. A name is looked up, not placed. `tests/e2e.py` reads the row labels and holds them in alphabetical order.

## 1.79.31

**Words the fourth critique read.** Six of them, each a small thing said the wrong way. The filter chips printed the state's field names — *search: zzqx*, *assignee: Alex Rivera* — under a row whose labels read FIND and PERSON; a chip now uses the row's word. A KPI's drill-down opened on *Delivered — 55% (12 of 22 items · 41 pts) · 12 issues, 41 points*, the panel appending its own count to a line that already stated it; the panel adds its count only when the opener's line does not. The footer said *rendered 9/4/2026, 4:56:33 PM* in the opener's locale and format under a page that writes *Aug 10* everywhere else; it and the tooltip's *Pulled* line are written the way the page writes a date, with the year and the time to the minute. *1 items still open* on the carry-over tile and *+1 items added* on the burndown are singular when they are one. In the wizard, *Could not read that text. no rows found* was a sentence followed by a parser's message; the notice reads its detail as a second sentence and starts it like one. And a header with nothing under it produced a mapping table that said *✓ matched* three times against nothing; a matched column with no rows says *no rows to read*. `tests/e2e.py` holds the chip's word, the single count on a KPI's panel, the footer's shape, the singular on a one-item dataset, the capitalised notice and the no-rows status.

## 1.79.30

**Small things the fourth critique read.** The Executive preset stranded *Release quality & speed* alone in a half-empty row at 1440, because the rule that gives a tile its partner's columns knew only the predictability chart and the forecast; it now knows both shared rows, so a preset or a copy that shows one of a pair without the other still sums every row to twelve, and `tests/e2e.py` sums the rows of both presets. The Tiles popover's heading was an `h4` sitting between the page's `h1` and the cards' `h2`s, the one heading skip on the page and the one thing the detector found that the reviewer had not; it and the Workflow popover's are `h2`. *Types* was the one filter label not set in small capitals, because it is a `span` rather than a `label`. The predictability axis said *S24* under a context bar that says *Sprint 24*; it says the name where the band is wide enough and abbreviates only where it is not. And at 1280 wide the toolbar still wrapped to two rows, 131px, not because of its buttons but because the title block's sub-line set a 439px width and would not yield; the block now shrinks to 300px and the sub-line wraps instead, and the test that held the toolbar under 110px at 1500 holds it at 1280 as well.

## 1.79.29

**On a phone the first figure is on the first screen.** The fourth critique measured a 375×812 phone: a 278px toolbar with six controls stacked, a 260px filter row, the health chip at 578px and the first KPI tile at 621px — or 794px with the context bar showing — so an executive opening the page on a phone saw a toolbar and a form and no number; the burndown's seven date labels also ran together as *Aug 1Aug 14*. Four changes below 760px. The source badge takes a line of its own above the buttons, which then fit one row. The Tiles button drops the preset and count it carries (its title keeps them, and the picker is one tap away). The six filters fold behind one line, *Filters · none active*, a button that says how many are on and opens the row; above 760px it is not rendered and the row is the row. The context bar keeps its three pickers, each on its own line, and loses the source chip and the dates that the title block directly above already states. And the burndown draws as many date labels as its width holds at 58px each, at most seven, rather than seven regardless. `tests/e2e.py` holds the first tile under 560px on a phone, and under 700px with the context bar; the fold's state, count and disclosure; the Tiles detail hidden with the title intact; no overlapping date labels; and that above 760px nothing of this applies.

## 1.79.28

**"In a file" is decided by whether anything answered, not by the protocol.** 1.79.23 turned the forecast and brief tiles off in a saved copy, and decided "saved copy" by `location.protocol`: `file://` was a copy, anything else had a connection. The fourth critique's reviewer served the built page from a plain static server, which is what an intranet folder or a wiki attachment does, and got both tiles on — the forecast card with an uppercase *HOW MANY ITEMS?* label and an empty input above *A forecast is not in this copy*, a control offered and then declined in the same breath. The page is now a file until the connection answers: both tiles are off in every preset, and when `probeLive()` gets its contexts back the preset the reader was on is resolved again with the connection in place, which brings the two tiles on — a view chosen tile by tile is left alone. Shown without a connection, the forecast tile prints its sentence with no control above it, and the record-fed tiles' *in this file* reads the same flag. Over the Forge bridge nothing visible changes: the bridge answers and the tiles come on with the first paint of the tenant's data. `tests/e2e.py` serves the page from a bare `http.server`, asserts both tiles are off, the view still reads *All sprint tiles* and the shown forecast has no input or segment control, then loads the same page from `serve_live.py` and asserts the connection brings both on with the preset name intact.

## 1.79.27

**The band's eight values sit on one baseline.** `DESIGN.md` calls the KPI band the executive's scan line and `PRODUCT.md` says that reader scans it left to right; the fourth critique measured the line and found three of them. A KPI tile is a `<button>`, browsers centre a button's contents vertically, and `display:block` on the children does not change that — so a sub-line that wrapped, or a delta that was present on one tile and absent on the next, moved the whole tile's contents: at 1440 wide the value tops ranged over 17px and the bars over the same, and *55%* and *3* on neighbouring tiles sat 9px apart. The tile is now a flex column — label at the top, value on one line, the bar pushed to the bottom with an auto margin and at least its old 8px above it — which is what a row of figures needs and what a button's default layout is not. `tests/e2e.py` reads every value's and every bar's top across the band at 1500 wide and holds each set within a pixel.

## 1.79.26

**The wizard's ■ warnings now stop Apply, and a bundle loads whole.** Two findings from the fourth critique's stress pass, both in step 3 of the import. First: a ■ *Duplicate keys … Counts will be inflated* warning in the red wash sat sixty pixels above a blue primary *Apply to the dashboard* that was live regardless — the design said stop and the button said go — and a file with a header row and nothing under it reached the same button reading *0 issues 0 done*. Any err-severity warning now turns Apply off, drops it to a secondary button, and prints the reason beside it in text — *Apply is off until this is fixed: Duplicate keys. 58 keys appear more than once …* — rather than carrying it in a dimmed button alone, which `DESIGN.md` forbids. An empty file is such a warning. Second: a multi-sprint bundle dropped on the wizard was flattened through the column mapping into one sprint of every issue on every board, under a step-2 sentence that said *This looks like a dashboard dataset, so the mapping is already exact*; the reviewer's page read *188 of 233 items are done*, *▲ 188 done vs 12 last sprint* and *a commitment 2030% above recent actuals*, with the context bar gone because `contexts[]` had been discarded. A JSON with `contexts[]` now skips the mapping and the sprint window — it carries its own, per sprint — and goes straight to a preview that says what it is, how many sprints and boards it holds, and that it replaces what is loaded; *Back* from there returns to the file step. `tests/e2e.py` pastes a header-only CSV and a duplicate-key CSV and asserts Apply is disabled with the reason beside it, then pastes `data/sample-bundle.json` and asserts the mapping step is skipped, the preview counts eighteen sprints, and the applied page has its context bar and its active sprint.

## 1.79.25

**"Replace everything" now replaces everything.** The fourth critique's reviewer pasted a two-row CSV over the demo, chose *Replace everything*, and read a page titled *Imported 2026-08-01 — delivery and value* that still said *Highpeak Commerce · Storefront Team*, still carried the demo's sprint goal, showed *▼ 1 done vs 12 last sprint* against five sprints of the demo's history, *11 releases per week ▲ improving*, and *v2.2.0 … Blocked by BLC-429 and BLC-441* — keys that were in no file the reader had loaded. `assemble()` in `src/import.js` started every new dataset from the one already loaded, in both modes, and inherited its `meta`, its `history`, its `releases` and its `dora`; each of those tiles is captioned *from the record, not the filtered issues*, which is precisely why a reader trusted them. This is the plausible-wrong-number class `CLAUDE.md` names as the worst failure the project has, and it landed on the one reader Principle 5 protects: whoever is sent the file. Under *Replace*, the new dataset now starts from what the file carries — a full JSON dataset brings its own blocks, a flat CSV brings none — and keeps only the organisation's calendar and currency, which are properties of the organisation and not of a file. Under *Merge* the loaded dataset is the base, as merging means, and the radio now says what it keeps. The tiles that read a record the file does not carry say so in the refusal callout with the clause the tools use: *No release record in this file … the evidence is absent, not noisy*, and the thin-history sentence says how many sprints the record holds. `tests/e2e.py` applies a fresh CSV over the demo and asserts the goal, the milestones, the release metrics and the *last sprint* delta are gone and each tile refuses; merges a value file over the demo and asserts they are kept; and pastes a JSON dataset with its own goal and release and asserts those are what appear. `docs/importing-data.md` no longer says releases and DORA are carried forward.

## 1.79.24

**The chrome above the band is no longer the tallest thing on the page.** The third critique measured it: a 175px toolbar on a 1440-wide screen and 218px at 1280, because an action cluster 1,221px wide — the source badge, the health chip and six buttons — wrapped to three rows beside a 640px title block, so the band [ADR 0032](docs/adr/0032-the-band-comes-before-the-verdict.md) had put first still began 270 to 313px down, and 697px down on a phone. Three moves. The sprint goal leaves the toolbar for the top of the verdict card, which is what it is context for. *Print* and *Export CSV* go behind one *More ▾* control, a menu with the same keyboard behaviour as the tile picker's Move menu. And the health chip leaves the toolbar for the right end of the KPI band: it is the verdict's shortest form, the verdict comes after the figures, and a summary of the band standing two containers above it was the one place the reading order was still the old way round. The toolbar is one row at desktop widths. Two smaller things with it: the band breaks to two rows at 1180px, where the grid halves, rather than at 1280, where the most common laptop width sat on the wrong side of the line and got two rows; and a tile without a delta keeps the delta's height, so a missing delta no longer makes its row a line shorter than the one beside it (rows still differ where a long sub-line wraps, which is content, not chrome). `tests/e2e.py` holds the toolbar under 110px and the band under 300px at 1500 wide, the chip's and the goal's new homes, the More menu's open, focus and Escape, one row at 1280 and the delta's reserved height at 1100. The ADR carries the amendment. The last of the third critique's issues.

## 1.79.23

**A saved copy no longer ends on a refusal addressed to a developer.** Two tiles have exactly one possible state in a file: the forecast, which is computed by the live connection, and the brief's recipients, which are configured in the app. Both rendered anyway, and what they rendered was for the wrong reader — *"It is run by `agent/tools/forecast.py` … Start it with `make serve-live` and reload"* and, as the last card on the page, *"The connection answered 404 for the recipients route."* — in the one build whose threat model is that it gets forwarded to someone who cannot run `make` and reads *404* as breakage. In a file both tiles are now off in every preset, so the default view is still *All sprint tiles* and not *custom*; the picker names them as hidden and says why — *off in a saved copy … tick either to see it say so*; and shown, the forecast says *"A forecast is not in this copy"* in the reader's words, with the tool's name and the make target moved behind the tile's help mark where the person who can act on them will look. The brief's status note prints only where a live connection answered. Over a connection — the Forge app, or a page served by `serve_live.py` — nothing changes: both tiles are on and fill in. `tests/e2e.py` holds the file's default view, the picker's sentence, the reader's copy, the help mark, the brief's silence about status, and that the loopback transport keeps both tiles on. The first of the third critique's two priority issues.

## 1.79.22

**Two silent caps, against a named rule.** The verdict's list of findings printed six of them and seven can fire; the seventh — *work in progress has risen while completion has not*, the one that names a systemic cause — was the one dropped, without a word, whenever all seven applied. The list has no cap now; one more line costs nothing and a truncated list reads as a complete one. The risk register printed nine rows and its critical rule fires once per stale highest-priority item, so a board with a dozen of them showed nine risks as if they were all. The nine stay — they are the most severe, because the rows are ranked — and the register now says *"Showing the 9 most severe of 14 risks"*, how many were not shown, that they are the same rules over other issues, and how to bring them up. Neither showed on the demo, which produces six findings and eight risks, which is how both outlived the rule in `CLAUDE.md` that names them. `tests/e2e.py` fires all seven findings and fourteen risks and holds both. From the third critique of 2026-09-04.

## 1.79.21

**Small things the third critique read.** The Move buttons 1.79.20 added rendered their label at 8px: the retired arrows' rule for icon buttons in that cell outranked the button's own size, and nothing showed it until the picker was opened. The rule is gone with the arrows. The picker's own note still said *"The arrows reorder the page"*; it says the Move menu does. The Workflow button's title read *"1 status this board uses are not named"*; the verb follows the count. The nine table toggles were named *"▤"* and the two close buttons *"✕"*, their purpose in a title only; each toggle is named *"Show <card heading> as a table"*, from the card's own heading as the help marks and the tables already are, and the close buttons are *Close*. And the Tiles popover was 761px tall with no bound — at a 720px viewport its *Default order* and *Save this view* were off-screen, and on a phone its left edge sat at −10px — so it is bounded to the viewport and scrolls, is a sheet on a phone, and its Move menus are placed from their buttons, fixed, so the scroll box cannot clip them. `tests/a11y.py` holds the label size and the names; `tests/e2e.py` holds the popover's bounds at 720px tall and 375 wide and the menu's escape.

## 1.79.20

**Reordering tiles is a Move menu, not thirty-six arrows.** The tile picker put an up and a down arrow beside each of its eighteen rows: thirty-six 22px tab stops, and putting a tile first was seventeen presses of one of them. Drag and drop was never the answer — on its own it puts the feature beyond a keyboard, which is the reason the arrows existed — so the replacement is the pattern Atlassian's own reorderable lists use inside Jira, where this page lives: one *Move ▾* button per row, opening a menu of four moves, *To top*, *Up*, *Down*, *To bottom*, with the moves a tile cannot make disabled rather than hidden. Enter opens the menu with focus on the first move the tile can make, arrows walk it, Enter makes the move, Escape closes it back onto its button; after a move the list rebuilds, the menu closes and focus returns to the tile's Move button, and the move is announced in the popover's live region as before. Eighteen tab stops instead of thirty-six, and *put this first* is one choice. `tests/e2e.py` drives all four moves through the menu; `tests/a11y.py` walks it from the keyboard alone, including Escape. The second of the two design decisions left by the evening critique of 2026-09-03.

## 1.79.19

**A page-level refusal, in the first tile.** Over an empty selection every issue-derived tile refuses in the callout, each in its own words, and the evening critique counted ten identical amber blocks in one viewport: each said a true thing and none of them read as the page's answer, while five record-fed tiles kept their figures beside them. The KPI band — the first tile since 1.79.12 — now carries the page-level statement: *"Nothing to report for this selection."*, its own sentence with the closing clause, then the cause in one sentence — *no issues match the current filters* over a search that emptied the page, *nothing is loaded yet* in the state a Forge tenant opens in, *nothing is loaded* in a file — and the one action that changes it, then a note that the tiles below speak for themselves and which tiles read the record rather than the issues. The tiles beneath keep their own words. This is not the banner [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) rules out, and the ADR now says why: what was ruled out was a banner *instead of* the tiles' sentences; a statement above them that names the cause keeps everything the page still knows. A modal was considered and rejected, because this is the state every new Forge tenant opens in and a dialog on every first visit is a door in front of the page. No digits in the band's statement, by the empty-selection digit sweep; the count stays in the basis line under the verdict. Pinned in `tests/e2e.py` in both empty states.

## 1.79.18

**Nothing priced is not zero value.** The *Value closed* tile printed *"$0"* over *"0 of 5 closed items priced"* in the dev tenant — a 27px figure that reads as *the sprint delivered nothing of value*, where the truth beneath it was *nobody has priced anything*, and the value card one row down already explained which of three reasons that was. A sum over no priced items is not a measurement, by the rule [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) applied to pace and scope this week: the tile prints a dash, keeps the honest count — *"none of 5 closed items priced"*, or *"nothing closed to price"* — and its tooltip says why a zero would be the wrong statement. The facts pack mirrors it: `value.closed_estimate` is null when `items_with_estimate` is zero and `value.value_withheld` carries the sentence, so the agent has nothing to quote as a figure; `items_without_estimate` stays, because it is a count. Pinned in `tests/e2e.py` and `tests/test_agent.py` on the sample with its values stripped. Seen in the screenshot of the 9.8.0 in-tenant check, 2026-09-04.

## 1.79.17

**Three things a screen reader, or a reader who flipped the measure, was not told.** The ▤ toggle on every chart produced a table with headers and no name — a screen reader arriving in it heard *Date, Remaining items, Plan items* and not what they were the headers of; each table view is now named from its card's own heading, *"Table view: Burndown, with scope changes shown"*, so a renamed card renames its table. The KPI band was eight buttons announced by their whole text in a row, with nothing before the first saying what the row was; it is a named group, *Headline numbers*, the same name the tile picker gives it. And the health chip read *"Needs attention (52/100)"* in items and *"(57/100)"* in points for the same sprint without saying which — the two volume components move with the measure, so the score does; it now says *"· in story points"* after the score whenever the reader has left the default, which is the moment it moved, and its working already said so on hover. `tests/a11y.py` holds the table names and the group; `tests/e2e.py` holds the chip's suffix appearing with the toggle and leaving with it. The evening critique's Sam and executive red flags, less the ones that need a design decision.

## 1.79.16

**A sprint that has not started has no scope growth either, and no sprint health yet.** 1.79.7 taught the page and the tools that before a sprint's first working day there is no clock, and withheld the pace. The evening's tenant screenshot showed what it had left behind: on the same not-started sprint the *Scope added* tile read *"0 · 0 items after kickoff · 0% growth"* and the health chip, built from *"3 of 4 measures"*, still carried scope stability at 100/100 for *"no mid-sprint additions"* — full marks for the absence of additions to a sprint that had no *after* yet. Scope growth is a share of what was committed before the sprint began, and before the first working day there is no *began*; it is the same fourth cause pace has, and it now joins the causes the component names: the tile reads *"— · not started · sprint starts 11 Sep"*, the risk register lists scope growth among the rules it could not run, the verdict withholds the sentence and says why, and the facts pack carries `scope.scope_withheld` beside a null `growth_pct`. With pace and scope both gone the score falls below half its weight and refuses, as [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) says it must: what was left was blockers and ageing, which describe hygiene, and a sprint that has not started has no sprint health to score. Pinned in `tests/e2e.py` and `tests/test_agent.py` on the same not-started fixture the pace fix uses.

## 1.79.15

**One focus ring, and a note inside a refusal a reader can see.** `DESIGN.md` said the 2px link-blue focus ring was on every focusable control and never removed. It was on seven kinds — the help marks, the health chip, the skip link and the brief form's fields and buttons — and the buttons, the KPI tiles, the icon buttons, the segmented toggles, the selects and the search field fell to the browser's default, so a keyboard reader met two focus languages on one page, with the weaker on the executive's band. One rule now names every kind of control, and `tests/a11y.py` focuses one of each and reads the ring back rather than trusting the stylesheet. And the note beneath the brief card's refusal — added in 1.79.10 to keep the connection's status on the page — was muted ink on the warn wash, which measures 4.3:1 in the dark theme; a note inside a refusal callout is secondary ink now, 8.7:1 dark and 7.3:1 light, and the suite holds it on the stylesheet directly in both themes, because the state that showed it needs a transport that answers 404 and the suite runs from a file. Both from the evening critique of 2026-09-03; with them, every priority issue that critique raised is closed.

## 1.79.14

**The tiles that read the record say so.** Five tiles draw from the sprint's own record rather than from the issues on screen: the burndown, the predictability chart, release quality, team load and releases. That is correct — a burndown series and a six-sprint history are facts about the sprint, not about whoever the person filter picked — and it was said nowhere. Under a search that emptied the selection, ten tiles refused in the callout while the burndown still said *"10 left"*, releases still said *"9/14 issues"* and one risk row still printed, beside a footer reading *"showing 0 of 22 after filters"*; the reader was told there was nothing here and shown figures in the same breath, with no mark on which cards a filter moves. Each of the five captions now ends *"From the sprint's record, not the filtered issues"* (the two release-fed ones say *the release record*), the one history-based risk says the same in its why, and the footer's filtered count names the five tiles that do not follow the filters. The DORA caption also loses *"industry-standard"*, the one piece of puffery on the page. `tests/e2e.py` holds the five captions and the footer under the emptying search. From the evening critique of 2026-09-03.

## 1.79.13

**The Tiles button says what it shows.** It read *"Tiles · Everything"* on a sprint board while the popover beneath it said *"4 hidden: How long finished work took, Work in progress, and how old it is, How much finishes each week, Where the work has been sitting"* — the flow tiles a sprint board does not draw. The count was already right in the popover and in the button's own hover title; the visible label was the one place the page said something that was not so, and it was the first control in the topbar of a product whose positioning is that it never does. The preset is named for what it shows on the board in view — *All sprint tiles* or *All flow tiles* — and the button carries the count whenever anything is hidden, whoever hid it: *"Tiles · All sprint tiles · 14 of 18"*, *"Tiles · Executive · 8 of 18"*, *"Tiles · Custom · 11 of 18"*, and plain *"Tiles"* only when every tile is on screen. The popover's preset buttons carry the same names. `tests/e2e.py` holds the button off the word *Everything* while anything is hidden and on the name and the count. From the evening critique of 2026-09-03.

## 1.79.12

**The band comes before the verdict.** The KPI band is the first tile under the filter row and *What this sprint means* the second. The order had been the other way since the verdict card was written, on the argument that a report opens with its conclusion, and it put the one row `PRODUCT.md` promises the executive — *"scans left to right across a single band; a figure buried … is a figure not read"* — fifth in reading order: measured at 717px from the top on a 1440-wide screen, behind a 175px topbar, the filter row and a 437px paragraph, and at 1,735px on a phone. Two independent critiques on 2026-09-03 named it, the evening one as its first priority issue. The verdict loses nothing by going second; every sentence in it restates a tile and adds the why, and read after the band it explains what was just scanned rather than asking the reader to hold six figures in prose until the tiles confirm them. The source order in `src/index.html`, the `TILES` list and the preset lists move together; `tests/e2e.py` holds the band first by name and inside the first screen at 1500 wide, and the executive and team views keep their shapes with the band leading. [ADR 0032](docs/adr/0032-the-band-comes-before-the-verdict.md) records the decision and what it rules out.

## 1.79.11

**The design system's ramps say what the page ships.** `DESIGN.md`'s YAML tokens are normative and its prose is context, and the two had drifted apart from the day the file was written: the prose named the 10px severity glyph, the 10.5px axis text, the 7px icon button, the 2–3px bars and the 9–10px drop zones, while the `typography` block listed seven roles and the `rounded` scale six steps, so Impeccable's detector reported twenty-five sizes and radii as *"off the DESIGN.md ramp"* on every scan — values the same document described a few lines below. Twenty-five findings that are all noise is a scan nobody reads, and the one real drift among them would go past unread with the rest. The frontmatter now carries an enumerated `typography.scale` of every size the page sets, named for where it is used, and a `rounded` ladder from the 2px bar to the 14px dialog; the sidecar carries the same ladder with purposes, and the toast's shadow, which was the one colour the detector could not place. The prose gains *The Enumerated Ramp Rule* — a new size or radius is added to the scale, with a name, before it is used — and its "three corner sizes and no more" is corrected to what it always meant: three sizes carry the page, above a short named ladder. The detector now reports nothing under the design-system rules; the two findings it still reports are the refusal callout's left rule, which is the signature component and documented as such. `/impeccable document`, scoped to the ramps: nothing in the visual system changed, only its description.

## 1.79.10

**No browser dialogs, and no status code offered as a sentence.** Five failures were reported with `alert()`: an unreadable file, an empty paste, unreadable pasted text, a required column left unmapped, and a sprint that could not be loaded over a live connection. A system dialog in a page whose whole voice is *we say what happened in a sentence*; announced to a screen reader as a nameless interruption; and invisible to every test in the suite, because Playwright dismisses dialogs silently — which is how four of them outlived the wizard's own warning block, which had been rendering the same class of message properly in step three since it was written. The wizard now has a notice slot on its first two steps, `role="alert"` so it is announced, filled in the block's own words and cleared by the next thing that works; the context bar carries a sprint that could not be loaded as its own refusal row, with the connection's reason verbatim, where the reader just acted. And the brief card, which printed *"The server did not answer about recipients (404)."* in any copy served by a plain static server, says what that is — a served copy with no recipient list, configured in the app against the site the data came from — and keeps the status in the note beneath, because it is still evidence; a resolver's own refusal is still printed verbatim. `tests/e2e.py` listens for dialogs now, so a sixth cannot arrive unnoticed, and drives all five paths. This was the last finding of the critique 1.79.0 names; its snapshot is closed with it.

## 1.79.9

**Two sentences a tenant read, corrected.** The flow-efficiency finding began every reading with *"Only"*, so above the target it printed *"Only 100% of elapsed time on closed items was spent actively working. The other 0% was queuing — waiting for review, for a decision, or for someone to be free"* over one closed item that had never waited — a good finding in a warning's words. *"Only"* now belongs to the warning alone, and a queue share of nothing is said as *"None of it was queuing"*, with the caution that it is rare and worth understanding before it is counted on. And the context bar's refresh button printed the connection's source id, *"Refresh from jira"*; it names the source now, *Jira* or *Asana*, and *"the server"* for anything else. Both from the first run of `tests/forge_smoke.py`; both pinned in `tests/e2e.py`.

## 1.79.8

**The seed names its source; the page states the state.** `forge/seed.json`'s label read *"this site's Jira — nothing loaded yet"*, and nothing replaced it when the bridge loaded a sprint, so a tenant's footer read *"Generated from this site's Jira — nothing loaded yet · 24 issues across 4 sprints · showing 18"*, and the basis line under the verdict said the same over eighteen issues. A label that carries a state goes stale the moment the state changes; the state was already the page's to say, and it says it — the refusal callouts over an empty board, *"Nothing loaded from this site's Jira"* in the basis line, *"showing 0"* in the footer. The label is *"this site's Jira"* now, and `tests/e2e.py` holds, over the loopback transport with the seed baked in, that once the connection has loaded a sprint nothing on the page says nothing is loaded. `tests/forge_smoke.py` holds the same inside the tenant. Found by that check's first run.

## 1.79.7

**A sprint that has not started no longer reads as fully elapsed.** The first run of `tests/forge_smoke.py` inside the dev tenant landed on a board whose next sprint began eleven days after the data's as-of date, and the page said *"The sprint is 100% elapsed, so delivery is behind the clock by roughly 72 percentage points"*, the pace tile read *"-72 pp"*, and the chip read **Off track (29/100)**. A verdict about delivery on a sprint in which no day had been worked, in the most prominent chip on the page, in a tenant's first view — the plausible-wrong-number class `CLAUDE.md` asks to be flagged loudly, and it is the worst instance of it this project has shipped, because it is the state every new board opens in until its first sprint starts. The cause was one expression on each side: `wd.indexOf(now)` in `derive()` and `wdays.index(as_of)` in `metrics.facts()` both fell through to *fully elapsed* for any as-of date not in the working-day list — before the first day, after the last, and every weekend in between, so a Saturday mid-sprint read the same. The page and the tools agreed with each other, which is what the agreement test checks, and were both wrong. Both now count the working days up to the as-of date: before the first there is no clock yet, and that is said rather than scored — the pace component leaves the health score by the drop-and-name rule of [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md), the tile reads *"not started · sprint starts 11 Sep"*, the verdict withholds its pace sentence and names the cause, and the facts pack carries `delivery.pace_withheld` with the same sentence for the agent to quote; after the last day it is all elapsed; on a weekend it is as far through as the working days already passed. `tests/e2e.py` and `tests/test_agent.py` pin the not-started, the Saturday and the after-the-end cases on their respective sides. Nothing else moved: the sample sprint's as-of is a Monday inside it, and every figure the two suites already held is unchanged.

## 1.79.6

**The deployed app is checked from inside the tenant.** A deploy proves the manifest is valid and the bundle builds; what a reader sees inside Jira — whether the bridge answered, which state the page opened in, whether the help layer rebuilt for the keyboard survived the split build and the iframe's content-security policy — was checked by a person clicking, because the Custom UI iframe is cross-origin and a browser extension's script cannot reach into it. `tests/forge_smoke.py` (`make forge-smoke`) drives a real browser instead, which is a different thing from running inside the page: it opens the app's project page on the dev site, finds the frame the dashboard rendered in by looking for the grid rather than for a hostname, and asserts from inside it — the context bar the bridge fills, a KPI band in one state and never a mix, no refusal in the note style, the stylesheet and every style attribute under the policy, the help marks and the health chip on focus, Escape, the landmark, the skip link, the table scopes, and a console with nothing in it but the inline-style reports the page produces by design, which are counted and printed rather than failed on or hidden. The sign-in is the operator's: the first run opens a visible browser with its own git-ignored profile and waits; later runs are headless on that session. It found two things on its first pass that the file-based suites could not: the first `focus()` into a cross-origin frame fires no focus event until the frame itself is focused, and the frame logs one policy report per style attribute the page writes, eighty-six on a first render — expected under the design recorded at the top of `src/app.js`, and now visible as a number rather than as a line nobody reads. Not part of `make test`: it needs a deployed environment and a person, and a suite that cannot run in CI must not look like one that did. `docs/forge-deployment.md` has the runbook.

## 1.79.5

**The help layer no longer needs a pointer.** Eighteen *i* marks, one beside every card heading and one beside the measure toggle, were `<span>`s that answered `mousemove` and nothing else; the health chip's working — the disclosure [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) made the thing a reader argues with — was a data attribute on a span nothing could focus; and the tooltip they all drew into, though it had `role="tooltip"`, was never named as anybody's description. For a keyboard, screen-reader or touch user the product's second principle failed on every tile at once: every figure had a basis, and none of it was reachable. The marks are buttons now, each named from its own heading (*"About Burndown, with scope changes shown"*) so a renamed card renames its help; focus shows the same tooltip hover does, anchored to the mark rather than the pointer; a click or tap toggles it, since a touch screen has no hover; the tooltip is announced as the mark's description for as long as it shows; Escape dismisses it; and a tooltip opened from the keyboard is not taken away by a pointer that happens to drift. The health chip is in the tab order and explains its score, or its refusal, on focus. Every KPI tile, already a button, now explains itself on focus too. The import dialog, which opened while focus stayed on the button behind the scrim, is a labelled modal dialog that takes focus on open and gives it back on close, exactly as the drill-down panel has since 1.20; its tabs name the panels they control. The page has a `<main>` landmark and a skip link to the report as its first tab stop, since fifty-odd controls stand between the top of the page and the first figure. Every table header declares whether it heads a column or a row. Two things the new checks turned up on the way: Escape, pressed anywhere on the page, ran the drill-down's close routine whether or not the panel was open, and its fallback moved focus to the first KPI tile — so a keyboard reader dismissing a tooltip was carried somewhere else; it returns at once when the panel is shut. And a click on a help mark arrives after the hover and the focus that already showed its tooltip, so a naive toggle closed on the first click; a click now toggles only against a tooltip a previous click opened. The table-view button says what the table is for: *the same figures, readable without the chart*. `tests/a11y.py` holds all of it — names, focus, description, dismissal, the dialog's focus round-trip, the landmark and the skip link. This was the P2 of the critique 1.79.0 names, the last of its findings above P3.

## 1.79.4

**The demo file agrees with itself.** `data/sample-sprint.json` is the sprint the built page opens on, and its last `history` row is that sprint's own record. The row was written by hand and disagreed with the sprint's issues on six fields: 13 items done against 12, 39 points against 41, 7 in progress against 6, 77 points committed against 74, a throughput of 13 against 12, and a flow efficiency of 0.34 against the 0.22 the page and the facts pack both compute. For as long as the *Delivered* tile's delta was a percentage the disagreement was invisible — 13 over the previous sprint's 8 is *"+63%"*, and nothing on screen said 13. 1.79.3 printed the counts, and the tile read *"12 of 22 items"* over *"13 done vs 8 last sprint"*. The row now carries what `metrics.history_row` derives from the issues, which is the same function the fetcher and the import wizard use to write a current row, and `data/sample-multi-sprint.json`, which tells the same sprint to the forecaster with twelve weeks of issues behind it, carries the same row. What moves on the page: the predictability chart's last bar and its hit rate for Sprint 24, and the work-in-progress series' last point; nothing derived from the issues changes, because it never read the row. `tests/test_agent.py` now holds the demo file's row against the tool's derivation field by field, so the file cannot drift from its own issues again. CONTRIBUTING's rule that derived data is recomputed, never inherited, had been applied everywhere except to the file that demonstrates it.

## 1.79.3

**Two KPI sub-lines say what their figure means.** The *Delivered* tile printed *"▲ +63% vs last sprint"* directly beneath *"55%"*. The headline is a share of the committed scope; the delta was the change in the count of items completed. Two denominators under one glyph, and a first-time reader takes it as the share having risen sixty-three points — the Points toggle turning it into *"+15%"* was the only tell that it compared counts. It now names both counts, *"12 done vs 7 last sprint"*, in the measure selected, so the comparison is legible without decoding it. The same code printed *"→ no change"* for a sprint with no predecessor, which is a comparison that was never made stated as its result; such a sprint now shows no delta at all. *Pace vs clock* was the only tile whose sub-line did not say its figure in words: *"-5 pp"* over *"60% elapsed · percentage points"*, the honest unit and the least legible one on the executive's band. The sub-line now reads *"60% elapsed · 5 percentage points behind the clock"*, which is the verdict's own phrasing one card up; the unit stays spelt out, because *"5 points behind"* beside an item measure reads as story points, and the tile's comment has said so since the unit was chosen. Both are pinned in `tests/e2e.py`, along with the absence of *"no change"* over a file with a single sprint in it. Found by the critique 1.79.0 names; these were the executive-reader red flags. **Naming the counts exposed a fault the percentage had hidden.** The demo file's *Delivered* tile reads *"12 of 22 items"* from its issues and, one line below, *"13 done vs 8 last sprint"* from its recorded history row, because `data/sample-sprint.json`'s row for Sprint 24 was written by hand and disagrees with the sprint's own issues on four fields. The comparison is the sprint record on both sides deliberately — a person filter must not move it, and the previous sprint exists only as a record — so the fix is to the file, in 1.79.4, not to the tile.

## 1.79.2

**A phone no longer opens on blank paper.** Every card header gives its title block `flex: 1 1 220px` so that it yields to the tools cluster before either wraps; 220px is a width, and it is the right one. Below 760px the header stacks the title over the tools, the main axis turns vertical, and the same basis became a height: on a 375px screen all thirteen visible headers were exactly 220px tall over forty to ninety pixels of heading, about 1,900px of paper in a 10,700px page, and the first figure sat more than a screen below the fold. The executive this page is written for reads it from an email on a phone, and the page read as broken before it read as anything. The basis is `auto` inside the stacked breakpoint; nothing else moves. `tests/e2e.py` measures every visible header at 375px and holds the gap between the box and the bottom of its last child, so a regression fails on what it looks like rather than on a pixel count that moves with the copy. Found by the critique 1.79.0 names.

## 1.79.1

**The basis line names the right cause, and the footer counts what survived the filter.** Two states reach the page's empty-selection refusal: a file or a tenant with no issues in it, and a filter that excluded every issue there was. The refusal is the same sentence for both, correctly, and the basis line beneath the verdict said *"Nothing loaded from Demo data"* for both — over twenty-two loaded issues that a search for `zzqx` had excluded. A reader who believes that sentence goes to check the connection; the fix was one click away in the filter row. It now says *"No issues match the current filters among the 22 loaded from …, as at 10 Aug. Clear a filter to bring them back."* when there were issues to exclude, and keeps *"Nothing loaded"* when there were not. This is the failure [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) describes for the health score's disclosure, one card up. The footer had the same fault in a number: *"showing 22"* beside a selection with nothing in it, because it printed the count the filter was applied to rather than the count that survived it. It reads *"showing 0 of 22 after filters"* now, and simply *"showing 22"* when nothing is filtered, so an unfiltered footer is unchanged. Also in this entry, three captions that named a measure the page was not using: the per-person chart's caption said *"Story points per person"* whichever measure was selected, over bars that read *"6 items"*; it follows the measure now, as its bars and the burndown's legend already did, and the burndown's and per-person chart's help text say *"in the measure selected in the filter row"* rather than committing to points. Each has a check in `tests/e2e.py`; the critique that found them is the one 1.79.0 names.

## 1.79.0

**Every refusal on the page is set in the refusal callout.** The words were already right: nine tiles refuse over an empty selection in the same sentence, ending *the evidence is absent, not noisy*, and `tests/e2e.py` holds the clause. The type contradicted them. The forecast tile printed its refusal in the callout `DESIGN.md` names as the page's signature component — a 3px rule in warn ink on warn wash — while the KPI band, the ageing, value and risk tiles and the per-person chart printed theirs as an 11.5px muted `.note`, and the verdict as bold text with no mark at all. In the state a Forge tenant opens in, the executive's scan line was the smallest, greyest text on the page, inside a full-width card, beside four charts that kept drawing; it read as a page that had half-broken rather than as a page that was speaking. [ADR 0010](docs/adr/0010-an-empty-selection-is-a-refusal.md) rules out dimming because opacity is the one channel that cannot carry a reason; muted grey at 11.5px is the same channel reached through typography. One class, `.refusal`, now carries every refusal, with the `.fc-refusal` name kept as an alias so the forecast tile's sixteen call sites need not move; the verdict's keeps the verdict's size. Where a tile's sentence is a refusal in one branch and a finding in the other — *no work in progress to age* over nothing, against *nothing is open in this selection* over issues that exist — only the refusal takes the callout, because a true finding is not a warning. The per-person chart gains the refusal sentence it lacked over an empty selection; with issues present and none assigned it keeps its own. The suite asserts each figure tile's refusal sits in the callout, that no `.note` anywhere on the page carries the clause, and that the KPI band's refusal is no longer below 12.5px. Found by an Impeccable critique of the page on 2026-09-03, whose snapshot is in `.impeccable/critique/`.

## 1.78.4

**The calculator's cloud is gone, and so is the last file that named it.** On 2026-09-03 the Cloud Run services were shut down and `wasm-probe-2`, the second throwaway app, was deleted in the developer console; Google Cloud project `calculator-506614` no longer answers the CLI. `.gcp-deploy.env`, the local state the provisioning script wrote and the teardown needed, is deleted with its `.gitignore` entry. What remains of the calculator in this repository is the record: [ADR 0008](docs/adr/0008-forge-calls-a-hosted-calculator.md), [0012](docs/adr/0012-the-calculator-is-reached-by-invokeremote.md) and [0030](docs/adr/0030-the-manifest-commits-to-its-hostnames-and-realms-once.md), `docs/hosting-the-calculator.md`, and the entries from 1.15.0 to 1.78.1 that built it, ran it and retired it.

## 1.78.3

**The connection check is gone.** The admin page that made the three calls a deploy leaves untested — a resolver reached from a static resource, one board read under the product's scopes, one issue's projection shown rather than asserted — was written to be deleted once the real bridge existed, and was kept two versions longer because it was the only thing that displayed an outbound payload. There is no outbound payload now: the calculator is retired, the projection is held by the suite against the resolver's own fixtures, and the two things it diagnosed for a person, an unconsented scope and a site with no story-point field, are reported in the product page's footer. Gone together, as the runbook said they should be: `forge/probe/`, the `connection-check` module, the `probe` resource, the `ping`, `probeBoardIssues` and `facts` resolvers, and `compute` and `fetchBoardIssues`, which only `facts` called. The `facts` route itself is unchanged; the brief still asks it. Removing a module is classified by deploying, and this one deployed as a minor version, **9.2.0**. The knowledge graph in `graphify-out/` was rebuilt over the tree so its communities describe the repository as it is.

## 1.78.2

**A test that held the migration's overlap has nothing left to overlap.** `test_routes_move_one_at_a_time` was written for the weeks in which some routes were answered inside the Forge function and the rest still by the hosted calculator: it grepped `index.js` for both sets and held that they did not intersect, and that whatever was still remote was a route the service declared. Since 1.78.0 there is no `callCalculator` in the file, so the remote set was empty and both checks passed vacuously, one of them under the name *"the calculator still answers what has not moved, until it goes"* on every run after it had gone. A check that cannot fail is a line in the output that reads as evidence and is not. The two are removed, along with a per-resolver grep for the same identifier that the sequence resolver's hand-over check carried; the scope block's *"nothing in the resolver reaches a remote"* already holds the absence file-wide, and one assertion is easier to keep true than three. What the test still holds is the inventory: every route `index.js` answers is written down in `IN_FUNCTION_ROUTES`, and every inventoried route is one `routes.py` declares. Found by a knowledge-graph trace of what still depended on the retired service, which turned up a test whose name described a state the tree no longer had.

## 1.78.1

**The hosted service's own files go.** 1.78.0 stopped calling the calculator; this deletes it: `service/app.py` with its socket and two auth verifiers, the Dockerfile, `requirements.txt`, the smoke test and scan gate, the provisioning script, the `container` job in CI and the two `pip` steps that installed and audited the service's one dependency, and `make serve-calc`. `service/` is `routes.py` and a README now, and `tests/test_service.py` — named for what it used to test, and kept so that nothing that points at it moves — asserts exactly that, alongside the checks it always made against the routes: the projection loses nothing, the answer is the tool's, refusals are sentences. The tests of the retired thing went with it: the auth seam, the token verifier and its forged, expired and rotated tokens, the Dockerfile reconstruction, the startup guard, the Debian security-update line. `call()` keeps its old signature and answers through `routes.answer` so the projection and refusal checks did not have to change. The security suite's two reads of `app.py` become one assertion that nothing hosted is left to authenticate to. `CLAUDE.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `README.md`, `forge/README.md` and `docs/forge-deployment.md` say what is now true, with the retired sections kept and marked rather than rewritten, because they are the record of what was built.

## 1.78.0

**The remote is gone, and with it the calculator: every figure the app shows is computed inside the Forge function.** The last commit of the series [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md) ordered. With `burndown` moved in 1.77.5 nothing named the calculator any more, so the `remotes` block and its three Cloud Run hostnames leave the manifest, `invokeRemote` and `callCalculator` leave the resolver, and `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` — the three-region deploy with its realm guard — is deleted, since there is nothing to deploy. Removing this remote is a **major version**, and the second probe had said otherwise: its remote was a bare `baseUrl`, and this one declared realms and `operations: [compute]`, which the linter names as *"data residency or egress modification"* in both directions. Checked by deploying, as `CLAUDE.md` says to: `forge deploy` stopped for `MAJOR_VERSION_RULE`, the approval was given with the only installation still the dev site, and 8.9.0 became **9.0.0** with `forge install --upgrade` re-consenting the installation. The deploy reports the app **eligible for *Runs on Atlassian***, which was the point. 9.0.0 shipped for the eleven minutes it took to notice that the deletion of the dead helper had started at the wrong comment and taken `answerHere` with it — esbuild bundles an undefined identifier without a word, and every route would have thrown inside the tenant. **9.1.0** restored it, and the suite gains a general guard: every bare call in the resolver must name something the file defines, imports or the runtime provides. `tests/test_service.py` inverts the assertions that held the remote's declaration against the code that reached it: no `remotes` block, no egress permission, no `invokeRemote`, and no `.run.app` hostname anywhere under `forge/`, because a remote added after the first external install is a major version, a reinstall for every tenant and the badge gone. [ADR 0012](docs/adr/0012-the-calculator-is-reached-by-invokeremote.md) and [ADR 0030](docs/adr/0030-the-manifest-commits-to-its-hostnames-and-realms-once.md) are marked superseded, and `docs/hosting-the-calculator.md` is kept as the record of what ran. The hosted service's own files — `service/app.py`, its Dockerfile, container gates and provisioning script — go in the next entry, because deleting a service is a different change from ceasing to call it.

**What is left to delete is in Google Cloud, and the CLI cannot do it from here.** Project `calculator-506614`: three Cloud Run services, three Artifact Registry repositories, the shared-secret entry in Secret Manager, the deploy and runtime service accounts and the workload identity federation `service/provision-gcp.sh` created, then the four repository variables in GitHub that pointed the deleted workflow at them. In that order:

```bash
for r in us-central1 europe-west3 europe-west2; do
  gcloud run services delete calculator --region="$r" --project=calculator-506614 --quiet
  gcloud artifacts repositories delete calculator --location="$r" --project=calculator-506614 --quiet
done
gcloud secrets delete calculator-shared-secret --project=calculator-506614 --quiet
gh variable delete GCP_PROJECT_ID; gh variable delete GCP_WIF_PROVIDER
gh variable delete GCP_DEPLOY_SA; gh variable delete GCP_RUNTIME_SA; gh variable delete FORGE_AUDIENCE
```

The service accounts and the federation pool can go with the project itself once nothing else is in it. The £75-a-month ceiling on calculator hosting falls away with them.

## 1.77.5

**The burndown is answered inside the Forge function, and nothing reaches the calculator any more.** The last route to move under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md): `/v1/burndown` is one sprint's daily series, bounded by the sprint's days, and the `context` resolver's one call to it now goes to `answerHere`. With it, every figure the app shows a tenant is computed by the Python inside the function — facts, the forecast and sequencing as jobs, the trend series, the burndown — and `callCalculator` has no caller left. It stays in the file for exactly one more commit, with the `remotes` block, the `invokeRemote` import and the hosted service's deployment, because retiring a remote is its own change to blame and this one is a route moving. Deployed to development as **8.9.0**, a minor version; the burndown tile on a sprint with days behind it is the click.

## 1.77.4

**The trend series is answered inside the Forge function.** The fifth route to move under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md), and a plain call: `/v1/history` is one row of counts per sprint, bounded by the number of sprints, and the `context` resolver's two calls to it — the series for the selected context, and the re-read after a recordable row is stored — now go to `answerHere`. Nothing but the identifier at two call sites changed; the inventory in `tests/test_service.py` gains the route. What remains on the calculator is `burndown`, one call, also from the `context` resolver. Deployed to development as **8.8.0**, a minor version; the sprint trend tile is the click.

## 1.77.3

**The forecast is a job, because the forecast on a real board does not fit a resolver call.** 1.77.2 moved it in-function synchronously, and the first click on the dev site failed with *"the request to the live server failed"*: the adapter's fifteen seconds ran out. Three instrumented deploys found where the time went — the forecast call alone was 12 to 15 seconds under the snapshot runtime, cold or warm, with memory (already 1,024 MB; the container reports 1,192 MB and two CPUs) and the Node version (24 saves one second) ruled out by deploy — and a native profile found why: the dev board has 906 working-day observations, most of them zero, and 31 open items, so `forecast_completion` walks about 280 days per trial and 20,000 trials is some five million draws, 1.3 s natively against 0.1 s on a dense board. The second probe measured a dense synthetic board, and [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md)'s *"facts and the forecast stay synchronous"* was wrong for any team with a long, sparse history; the record now carries a dated correction with the figures.

**The same machinery, generalised rather than duplicated.** `forge/src/jobs.js` knows two kinds. The `forecast` resolver still chooses the slice synchronously, which is cheap, fetches the issues and reads the calibration log as before, then hands the request to `startJob`, the one function both resolvers now call; the consumer, renamed `simulationConsumer` on `simulation-fn` draining the `simulations` queue, runs whichever of the two allow-listed routes the event names, on the plain runtime; and `jobResult`, which replaces `sequenceResult`, finishes a forecast at collection by doing the two things the resolver used to do after the call — the at-risk items' titles and assignees re-read from Jira by key as the collector, bounded by the risk list and keeping a team's titles out of storage, and the calibration log written if it moved. The adapter polls both routes and the page is untouched. One consequence was designed in rather than met: a forecast's payload is a team's whole history, 130 issues on the dev board and thousands on a real one, past the event's 100 KB limit that sequencing never approached, so the projection now travels through app storage in chunks of 160 KB, written under their own id before the push so they exist when the consumer starts within a second of it, and deleted by the consumer as it reads them. One path for both kinds, capped at the hosted service's own 4 MB with a sentence above it; a 4,000-issue board is seven rows of 192 KB. Four stores are declared in the permission-mirroring inventory with what each exposes. `tests/jobs_shapes.mjs` gains the forecast kind, the chunk round trip and the short-payload throw; `tests/test_service.py` holds the manifest against the code, the order of the guards in `startJob`, both resolvers handing over rather than computing, the collector's re-read as the user of summary and assignee only, and the adapter driven on both routes to its ceiling. Also kept from the diagnosis: `nodejs24.x`, which Atlassian recommends and the probe measured on, the memory declared app-wide as well as per function, and the forecast resolver's phase timings in the log, milliseconds and counts only. Deployed to development as **8.7.0**, a minor version; the Monte Carlo tile is the next click.

## 1.77.2

**The forecast is answered inside the Forge function.** The third route to move under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md), and the one a reader actually clicks: the Monte Carlo tile's *When* and *How many* views. It is two calls, and both moved together because they are one question — the resolver asks `/v1/slice` which contexts a forecast for this one samples, fetches exactly those, and sends them to `/v1/forecast-context`; a slice chosen in-function and a forecast computed by the calculator would be two runtimes agreeing about a list, which is the kind of agreement nothing checks. The brief's forecast call moved with it, so nothing in `index.js` reaches the calculator for a forecast any more. Measured on the second probe, the context forecast is 1.5 s warm at 480 issues and 2.8 s at 4,800, and cold it rides on the snapshot load at about 6 s, inside the adapter's fifteen seconds. Nothing but the identifier at three call sites changed; the inventory in `tests/test_service.py` gains the two routes and holds that neither is still sent to the calculator. Deployed to development as **8.3.0**, a minor version. What remains on the calculator is `history` and `burndown`, both reached from the `context` resolver.

## 1.77.1

**The facts route is answered inside the Forge function.** The second route to move under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md), and the first that keeps its shape: `/v1/facts` answers in a third of a second warm, so it moves as a call and not as a job. `answerHere(path, body)` is `callCalculator`'s drop-in — the same route names and bodies, the envelope on success and `{available: false, sentence}` with the route's own sentence on a refusal — backed by `runtime.answer` from the memory snapshot, so a caller changes one identifier. Both callers of the route changed it: the `facts` resolver, which the page never asks and the connection check was built around, and the scheduled brief's `boardFigures`, which is why `weekly-brief-fn` now declares the same 1,024 MB the other two functions do. The brief's forecast call stays with the calculator until its own commit. What the migration must not produce is a route answered both ways in one deploy, and `tests/test_service.py` now holds an inventory of the routes that have moved against the code: the set `answerHere` is called with must equal the inventory, must not overlap the set `callCalculator` is still called with, and both must be routes `routes.py` declares. Deployed to development as **8.2.0**, a minor version; nothing on the page exercises the route, so the click that validates it is a brief run.

## 1.77.0

**Sequencing runs inside the Forge function, as a job, and the page cannot tell.** The first route to move under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md), and the one that had to change shape to do it: sequencing is cubic in the ask count and twelve asks is three and a half minutes on Forge's CPU, so no synchronous resolver answers it. The `sequence` resolver now assembles the asks and the projection exactly as before, validates the request through `/v1/sequence-check` — the same `check_sequence` the route runs, executed in-function by the Python runtime loaded from its memory snapshot, so the cap and every refusal sentence have one implementation — refuses a payload over the platform's 100 KB event limit rather than truncating it, pushes one event to the `sequence-jobs` queue, writes a job row bound to the asking account, and answers 202 with a job id. A new consumer function, `sequence-fn` under a 900-second, 1,024 MB budget, reads Forge's retry context before it loads anything and on any retry writes a refusal and returns — measured, a consumer killed at its timeout is re-invoked forty seconds later, for a day — then loads the runtime plain, because on Forge everything after a snapshot load runs 1.65× slower, runs `/v1/sequence`, and writes the envelope to a result row. A `sequenceResult` resolver collects: 202 while the consumer runs, the tool's answer with the titles and bases joined back on when it is done, a refusal when it was refused, and a 404, 403 or 410 for a job that is gone, another account's, or older than an hour by its own timestamp, with both rows and the in-flight key deleted the moment a final answer is handed over. A reload mid-job joins the running job: the in-flight key is a hash of the context, the projection and the account, and a second request for it collects rather than pushes. The adapter in `forge/bridge/bridge.js` is where the polling lives, because it is the one script permitted to know what Forge is: a 202 from `sequence` is polled through `sequenceResult` at two, three and then five-second intervals for up to ten minutes, and the page gets the one `{status, body}` it always got. `src/app.js` is untouched.

**Where the customer's words go, and where they do not.** The event carries the projection and the ask ids, never a title or a basis. Those are held in the job row — the app's own storage inside the tenant, the same shelf that holds a board's recipient list — for the life of the job, and joined on at collection by the account that asked. Three new stores are declared in the permission-mirroring inventory in `tests/test_service.py` with what each exposes, and the poll's account comparison is what makes ADR 0018's mirroring hold here by a check rather than by the entropy of a job id.

**What holds it.** `forge/src/jobs.js` is the pure half — the keys, the guards, the sentences and the state machine a poll walks — with no SDK in it, and `tests/jobs_shapes.mjs` runs it under Node over a matrix of twelve rows that `tests/test_service.py` holds outcome by outcome: the status, the body, and whether the rows are deleted. The same test holds the manifest against the code (the queue named once in `jobs.js` and once in the manifest are compared as strings, because an event pushed to a queue nobody consumes is accepted by the platform and run by nothing), the order of the resolver's guards, the consumer's retry guard ahead of its runtime load, `/v1/sequence-check` refusing five bad bodies byte for byte as `/v1/sequence` does, and the adapter's poll driven with a fake transport: the back-off, the ceiling, and that a refusal or a 404 is handed straight to the page. The parity suite gains the check route. Both functions declare 1,024 MB, because the resolver function now loads the runtime too and its measured footprint is 347 MB against a 512 MB default. Deployed to development as **8.1.0** — a minor version, as the second probe's deploy said a consumer module and a second function would be, with no approval stop — and the sequencing tile on the dev site is the next click. The deploy still reports the app ineligible for *Runs on Atlassian*, which is the `remotes` block, and stays so until the last commit of this series removes it.

## 1.76.2

**A sixth suite: the same Python under WebAssembly, byte for byte.** `tests/test_wasm.py` is the successor of the hosted service's parity test and the only thing standing between two runtimes and a figure that drifts between them unnoticed. It regenerates `forge/src/assets.js` with the generator a deploy uses, so it never tests last deploy's Python; answers every route the service suite exercises natively through `routes.answer` — facts, both forecasts, the slice, history, the burndown, an ask, a four-ask sequencing and four refusals — serialised with sorted keys; then answers the same bodies through `forge/src/runtime.js`, the loader the function ships, in one Node process per load mode, from the snapshot as the resolver function will and plain as the consumer will; and compares the strings. On this machine every one of the fourteen cases matches under both loads, the snapshot loads in 170 ms against 1.1 s plain, and four asks sequence in 2.2 s. It also asserts every `.py` in `agent/tools/` is in the bundle, because a module added there that the generator missed would import natively and fail inside a tenant. `make test-wasm` runs it, `make test` includes it, and CI runs it on every push with the pinned `pyodide` package cached by the lockfile under Node 24, which is Forge's. One finding on the way: the memory snapshot makes every generated bundle's digest new, so the loader now removes earlier bundles' directories when it unpacks — a container runs one bundle, and `/tmp` is 512 MB.

## 1.76.1

**The Python runtime travels into the Forge function as a generated module, and the loader that unpacks it exists.** `make forge-assets`, which `make forge-deploy` and `make forge-lint` now depend on, runs `forge/build-assets.mjs`: it reads the five files of the pinned `pyodide` package — an exact `314.0.6` in `forge/package.json`'s devDependencies, because a snapshot made by one Pyodide and loaded by another is undefined — every `.py` in `agent/tools/` and `service/routes.py`, writes them gzip-and-base64 into `forge/src/assets.js`, and adds a memory snapshot taken after the sources are imported. Sixteen megabytes encoded, one second to make. The file is git-ignored and the security suite asserts the line, for the reason [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md) gives: it is a second copy of the Python, and a second copy drifts. `forge/src/runtime.js` is the other half — unpack once per container into a directory keyed by the bundle's digest, import Pyodide through a `Function` the bundler cannot rewrite, load from the snapshot or plain by a flag, and `answer(path, body)`, which hands the route and a JSON body to `routes.answer` and returns the string Python serialised with sorted keys. The boot sequence the snapshot is taken after is one string, exported by the loader and imported by the generator, so the snapshot and a plain load cannot have imported different things. Nothing in the resolver calls any of this yet; that is the next commits, one route each.

## 1.76.0

**The calculator's answers are one module, and the socket in front of them is another.** The first code change under [ADR 0031](docs/adr/0031-the-forecast-runs-inside-the-forge-function.md). `service/app.py` held two things that only ever travelled together by accident: the projection, the caps, the refusals and the eight route functions, which are what a Forge function has to run under WebAssembly; and HTTP, the two auth verifiers and the body limit, which stay behind with the hosted service until it is retired. The first half is now `service/routes.py`, ending in `answer(path, body)` — the envelope with no socket and no auth — and `app.py` imports it, re-exports every name by name and adds nothing to any envelope. A new check in `tests/test_service.py` holds that literally: every route, refusals included, answers the same bytes through `handle()` as through `answer()`, every `app.route_*` is the same object as `routes.route_*`, and `routes.py` imports nothing a WebAssembly runtime lacks. The Dockerfile copies the new file by name, and the smoke test that reconstructs the image from its `COPY` lines is what would have caught forgetting to.
